[tool-240] exec_command -> === chelpers ===
[tool-240] exec_command -> '''
[tool-240] exec_command -> Cython functions for space curve analysis.
[tool-240] exec_command -> '''
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command -> import numpy as n
[tool-240] exec_command -> cimport numpy as n
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command -> cimport cython
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command -> from libc.math cimport abs, pow, sqrt as csqrt, floor
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command -> cpdef find_crossings(double [:] v, double [:] dv,
[tool-240] exec_command ->                      double [:, :] points,
[tool-240] exec_command ->                      double [:] segment_lengths,
[tool-240] exec_command ->                      long current_index,
[tool-240] exec_command ->                      long comparison_index,
[tool-240] exec_command ->                      double max_segment_length,
[tool-240] exec_command ->                      long jump_mode=1
[tool-240] exec_command ->                      ):
[tool-240] exec_command ->     '''
[tool-240] exec_command ->     Searches for crossings between the given vector and any other
[tool-240] exec_command ->     vector in the
[tool-240] exec_command ->     list of points, returning all of them as a list.
[tool-240] exec_command ->                      
[tool-240] exec_command ->     Parameters
[tool-240] exec_command ->     ----------
[tool-240] exec_command ->     v0 : ndarray
[tool-240] exec_command ->         The current point, a 1D vector.
[tool-240] exec_command ->     dv : ndarray
[tool-240] exec_command ->         The vector connecting the current point to the next one
[tool-240] exec_command ->     points : ndarray
[tool-240] exec_command ->         The array or (x, y) values of all the other points
[tool-240] exec_command ->     segment_lengths : ndarray
[tool-240] exec_command ->         The length of each segment joining a point to the
[tool-240] exec_command ->         next one.
[tool-240] exec_command ->     current_index : long
[tool-240] exec_command ->         The index of the point currently being tested.
[tool-240] exec_command ->     comparison_index : long
[tool-240] exec_command ->         The index of the first comparison point
[tool-240] exec_command ->     jump_mode : int
[tool-240] exec_command ->         1 to check every jump distance, 2 to jump based on
[tool-240] exec_command ->         the maximum one, 3 to never jump and check the length
[tool-240] exec_command ->         of every step.
[tool-240] exec_command ->     '''
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef list crossings = []
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double vx = v[0]
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double vy = v[1]
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double vz = v[2]
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double dvx = dv[0]
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double dvy = dv[1]
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double dvz = dv[2]
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double twice_max_segment_length = 2*max_segment_length
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef int i = 0
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double distance, distance_travelled
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double [:] point
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double [:] next_point
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double jump_x, jump_y, jump_z
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double pz
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double dpz
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef long intersect
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double intersect_i, intersect_j
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double crossing_sign
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef double crossing_direction
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef long jumps
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef long num_jumps
[tool-240] exec_command ->     cdef int already_jumped = 0
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command ->     while i < len(points) - 1:
[tool-240] exec_command ->         point = points[i]
[tool-240] exec_command ->         distance = csqrt(pow(vx - point[0], 2) + pow(vy - point[1], 2))
[tool-240] exec_command ->         if distance < twice_max_segment_length or already_jumped:
[tool-240] exec_command ->             already_jumped = 0
[tool-240] exec_command ->             next_point = points[i+1]
[tool-240] exec_command ->             jump_x = next_point[0] - point[0]
[tool-240] exec_command ->             jump_y = next_point[1] - point[1]
[tool-240] exec_command ->             jump_z = next_point[2] - point[2]
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command ->             intersect, intersect_i, intersect_j = do_vectors_intersect(
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 vx, vy, dvx, dvy, point[0], point[1],
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 jump_x, jump_y)
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command ->             if intersect:
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 pz = point[2]
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 dpz = jump_z
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 crossing_sign = sign((vz + intersect_i * dvz) -
[tool-240] exec_command ->                                      (pz + intersect_j * dpz))
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 crossing_direction = sign(cross_product(
[tool-240] exec_command ->                     dvx, dvy, jump_x, jump_y))
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 crossings.append([<double>current_index + intersect_i,
[tool-240] exec_command ->                                   (<double>comparison_index + intersect_j +
[tool-240] exec_command ->                                    <double>i),
[tool-240] exec_command ->                                   crossing_sign,
[tool-240] exec_command ->                                   crossing_sign * crossing_direction])
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 crossings.append([(<double>comparison_index + intersect_j +
[tool-240] exec_command ->                                    <double>i),
[tool-240] exec_command ->                                   <double>current_index + intersect_i,
[tool-240] exec_command ->                                   -1. * crossing_sign,
[tool-240] exec_command ->                                   crossing_sign * crossing_direction])
[tool-240] exec_command ->             i += 1
[tool-240] exec_command -> 
[tool-240] exec_command ->         elif jump_mode == 3:
[tool-240] exec_command ->             i += 1  # naive mode - check everything
[tool-240] exec_command ->             already_jumped = 1
[tool-240] exec_command ->         elif jump_mode == 2:
[tool-240] exec_command ->             num_jumps = <long>(floor(distance / max_segment_length)) - 1
[tool-240] exec_command ->             if num_jumps < 1:
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 num_jumps = 1
[tool-240] exec_command ->             i += num_jumps
[tool-240] exec_command ->             already_jumped = 1
[tool-240] exec_command ->         else:  # Catch all other jump modes
[tool-240] exec_command ->             distance_travelled = 0.
[tool-240] exec_command ->             jumps = 0
[tool-240] exec_command ->             while (distance_travelled < (distance - max_segment_length) and
[tool-240] exec_command ->                    i < len(points)):
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 jumps += 1
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 distance_travelled += segment_lengths[i]
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 i += 1
[tool-240] exec_command ->             if jumps > 1:
[tool-240] exec_command ->                 i -= 2
[tool-240] exec_command ->             already_jumped = 1

[tool-c5e] exec_command -> cpdef angle_exceeds(double [:, :] ps, double val=2*n.pi,
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->                     long include_closure=1):
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     '''Returns True if the sum of angles along ps exceeds
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     val, else False.
[tool-c5e] exec_command -> 
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     If include_closure, includes the angles with the line closing
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     the end and start points.
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     '''
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     cdef double angle = 0.
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     cdef double [:] nex = ps[0]
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     cdef double [:] nex2 = ps[1]
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     cdef double [:] dv2 = n.zeros(3, dtype=n.double)
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     diff(dv2, nex, nex2)
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     divide(dv2, mag(dv2))
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     cdef double [:] cur
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     cdef double increment
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     cdef long lenps = len(ps)
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     cdef long [:] checks = n.arange(len(ps)) if include_closure else n.arange(len(ps)-2)
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     cdef int i
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     for i in checks:
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->         cur = nex
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->         nex = nex2
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->         nex2 = ps[(i+2) % lenps]
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->         dv = dv2
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->         diff(dv2, nex, nex2)
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->         divide(dv2, mag(dv2))
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->         increment = angle_between(dv, dv2)
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->         if n.isnan(increment):
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->             return True
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->         angle += increment
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->         if angle > val:
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->             return True
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     assert not n.isnan(angle)
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     return False
[tool-c5e] exec_command -> 
[tool-c5e] exec_command -> cdef void diff(double [:] dv2, double [:] nex, double [:] nex2):
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     dv2[0] = nex2[0] - nex[0]
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     dv2[1] = nex2[1] - nex[1]
[tool-c5e] exec_command ->     dv2[2] = nex2[2] - nex[2]

Now, let me check the existing environment's numpy version more carefullyopia. The main issue with Numpy >= 2.0 is that the C API changed. The `np.int`, `np.double`, `n.isnan` etc are deprecated/removed in NumPy 2.0. Also, Cython-generated code from old Cython versions uses removed NumPy C API functions (like `PyArray_DescrFromType`) when `np.bool_`, or old buffer protocol stuff.

But wait - maybe there's a distutils issue with Python 3.13: distutils is removed in 3.12+! The setup.py uses `from distutils.extension import Extension`. In Python 3.12, distutils was removed from stdlib. So building may fail unless setuptools provides distutils shim. Actually setuptools vendors distutils when `SETUPTOOLS_USE_DISTUTILS=local` (default in recent setuptools). Since pip builds with setuptools, `from distutils.extension import Extension` should work through setuptools' vendored distutils. But when invoking setup.py directly, we'd need setuptools installed)Skip.

The pytest tests require `pytest`. Let me check if pytest is installed.

Now let's understand the main issue: numpy 2.0 removed the old deprecations:
- `np.int`, `np.float`, `np.bool`, `np.object`, `np.string_`, `np.unicode_`, `np.complex` are removed.
- `n.isnan` etc still exist though.
- `np.NaN`, `np.Inf` existed (np.NaN removed).
- The bigger issue: when Cython compiles code from `cimport numpy`, the pxd in old Cython versions generated references to old NumPy C API names like `PyArray_DescrFromType`, `PyArray_Type`, `npy_intp`... Actually Cython 3.3.0 ships `__init__.pxd` which handles numpy 2.0 fine – Cython 3.0+ supports NumPy 2.0.

Wait, Cython 3.3.0 supports numpy 2.x. The main issue with pyknotid's pyx files: they use `np.zeros`, `n.arange`, `np.int`, `np.double` etc. In NumPy 2.0:
- `np.double` still exists (alias float64). Actually `np.double` is still available in 2.0 and 2.3.
- `np.int` is removed! In ccomplexity.pyx: `indices = np.zeros(4, dtype=np.int)`. `np.int` was removed in NumPy 1.24 deprecation→ removal in 2.0. So this would raise at runtime (AttributeError) because the Cython-compiled code calls `np.zeros(4, dtype=np.int)` at module load? Wait, actually `indices = np.zeros(4, dtype=np.int)` is inside the function, only executed when the function is called. So module import would work but calling the function would fail with AttributeError: module 'numpy' has no attribute 'int'.

Let me check `np.double` in numpy 2.3: np.double is still there. `n.isnan` still exists.

Also `from libc.math cimport abs` — that's fine.

Now, the bigger compat issue: Cython needs to generate code that's compatible with NumPy 2.0's C API. Old Cython versions (<3.0) generate code using `PyArray_DescrFromType`, `PyArray_ISCONTIGUOUS` etc that were removed in NumPy 2.0. But with Cython 3.3, this is handled. Let me verify by compiling.

But here's another potential issue: `from pyknotid.utils import vprint` in cinvariants.pyx — that's a Python import in the pyx, fine.

Let me check pyknotid/utils.py and spacecurves/__init__.py to understand imports. Also check the .pyx import usage from Python side: `from pyknotid.spacecurves import chelpers` — since chelpers is a compiled extension, can you import it as a module from the package? Yes if it's compiled as a module pyknotid/spacecurves/chelpers.so.

[tool-7ec] exec_command -> - :doc:`spacecurve`: Provides functions for calculations on a single

[tool-7ec] exec_command -> - :doc:`knot`: Provides functions for topological calculations on a

[tool-7ec] exec_command -> - :doc:`openknot`: Provides functions for topological calculations on
[tool-7ec] exec_command ->   an open curve that does not form a closed loop. Open curves are
[tool-7ec] exec_command ->   topologically trivial from a mathematical perspective, but can be
[tool-7ec] exec_command ->   analysed in terms of the topology of different closures.
[tool-7ec] exec_command -> 
[tool-7ec] exec_command -> - :doc:`link`: Provides the same interface to collections of multiple
[tool-7ec] exec_command ->   curves, and can calculate linking invariants.
[tool-7ec] exec_command -> 
[tool-7ec] exec_command -> - :doc:`periodiccell`: Provides some convenience functions for

In planarity 1.0.0:
- `pg.nodes(include_drawplanar_vertex_info=True)` returns node attributes `vertex_position`, `vertex_start`, `vertex_end`.
- In older planarity versions (0.2.x), the attributes were named `pos`, `start`, `end`? Let me check the old planarity API. The old `networkx_graph` used attributes `pos`, `start`, `end` (matching pyknotid's expectations).

Let me check the planarity changelog: In planarity 0.2.3, `embed_drawplanar` stored:
- vertex_position → 'pos'?
- Actually let me check the old planarity source. The older versions used `edge_position`, `edge_start`, `edge_end`? Hmm.

The pyknotid `_construct_planar_graph` expects:
- `data['pos']` → y coordinate
- `data['start']` → x begin
- `data['end']` → x end

So in planarity 1.0.0, `vertex_position` should map to `pos`, `vertex_start` to `start`, `vertex_end` to `end`.

So the fix: after `pg.embed_drawplanar()`, we need to convert the drawplanar vertex info to the legacy names for pyknotid. Either:
1. Modify `_construct_planar_graph` in representation.py to map the new attribute names.
2. Or monkey-patch around.

The proper fix is to edit `pyknotid/representations/representation.py:_construct_planar_graph` to handle both naming conventions (for compatibility).

OK, no attributes set on g before passing to planarity. planarity.PGraph(g) computes drawplanar embedding; then networkx_graph(pg) returns a graph with new attribute names.

So the compat fix in `_construct_planar_graph` is to translate the attribute names. Let me also check whether old planarity versions' `networkx_graph` returned 'pos','start','end' — yes, pyknotid 0.5.3 was written for planarity 0.2.x which used those names. planarity 1.0.0 changed the names.

Let me look at how planarity 1.0.0 changed names: In planarity 1.0.0 release notes: I recall the `PGraph` node/edge info keys changed:
- Old (0.2.x): `pos`, `start`, `end` for both nodes and edges? Let me check the old version's behavior via git history or the planarity docs.

Let me search online knowledge: python-planarity 0.2.3 `networkx_graph` documentation: For nodes, attribute `pos` is the vertex position; `start`/`end` for edges... Actually I recall in planarity 0.2.x:
- `PGraph.nodes(include_drawplanar_vertex_info=True)` yields `(node, {'pos': ..., 'start': ..., 'end': ...})`?
Let me check the planarity GitHub CHANGELOG to confirm the names are equivalent.

From planarity 1.0.0 release notes (I recall): "the drawplanar vertex info keys from `pos`/`start`/`end` to `vertex_position`/`vertex_start`/`vertex_end`, and edge info from `pos`/`start`/`end` to `edge_position`/`edge_start`/`edge_end`". Yes, something like that.

So a robust fix: after obtaining the networkx graph, normalize attribute names from the new names to the legacy names, but also keep backwards compatibility (i.e., if keys already exist, don't overwrite). Something like:

```python
        def _convert_drawplanar_attrs(graph):
            node_renames = {'vertex_position': 'pos', 'vertex_start': 'start', 'vertex_end': 'end'}
            edge_renames = {'edge_position': 'pos', 'edge_start': 'start', 'edge_end': 'end'}
            for _, data in graph.nodes(data=True):
                for new_name, old_name in node_renames.items():
                    if new_name in data and old_name not in data:
                        data[old_name] = data[new_name]
            for _, _, data in graph.edges(data=True):
                for new_name, old_name in edge_renames.items():
                    if new_name in data and old_name not in data:
                        data[old_name] = data[new_name]
```

Wait — but the edge data may not contain 'start'/'end' keys in new planarity; edge keys are 'edge_position', 'edge_start', 'edge_end'. Let's verify with the actual trefoil graph. Let me run the failing test scenario to see the actual data keys.

There is a bare `from periodiccell import ...` in `periodic.py`. This is clearly a bug in the original package — it relies on running from within the directory so that `periodiccell` resolves as a top-level module. This is a Python-2-era relative import mistake (should be `from pyknotid.spacecurves.periodiccell import ...`).

Note: `periodic.py` is imported by `make/periodic_knot.py`, which is in the make package. This is a pre-existing issue; let's fix it to use the full module path.

Now numpy is back to 2.3.0. But wait — the pyknotid extension .so files in site-packages were built against numpy 2.5.2 headers. The numpy C API in 2.3 vs 2.5 — since Cython uses the stable ABI for numpy? No, numpy extensions aren't ABI-stable across minor versions necessarily; numpy promises backwards compatibility (an extension built against 2.5 headers should run against 2.3? Actually numpy C API is designed so that extensions built against newer numpy might not run with older numpy — you should build against the oldest numpy you want to support). There could be an ABI mismatch if built with a newer numpy header but run against older.

However, the actual runtime C API is accessed via `numpy/__init__.py` magic? No, numpy extensions link the numeric version via `#define NPY_TARGET_VERSION` etc. Built against 2.5.2 headers, it may reference symbols that exist in 2.3 (since 2.3 is older, 2.5 added symbols; but if it uses only old symbols, it runs fine). The concern is the reverse: building against newer and running on older is generally supported for minor version differences? Actually numpy states: "A module compiled against a newer NumPy may not run with an older NumPy." The safer approach: rebuild pyknotid against numpy 2.3.0 to be sure.

So we should rebuild the extensions in the site-packages installation with numpy 2.3.0. The cleanest approach: uninstall pyknotid, then `pip install .` (without force-reinstall) so numpy stays 2.3.0)Skip, and the build uses numpy 2.3.0 headers.

Again a bare `from coctree import ...` — relying on it being in the same directory. In the installed package, `pyknotid/simplify/coctree` is a submodule; the bare `from coctree import` would not work unless something makes `coctree` importable as a top-level module. But since it's inside a try/except that falls back to Python implementations, it doesn't error. Still, since we already have a compiled coctree extension, it would be cleaner to make this import work so the Cython octree functions are actually used. However, pyknotid's octree.py explicitly comments out the cython usage (`# angle_exceeds_func = (cangle_exceeds if ...)`) — so even if we fix the import, the cython functions aren't used by default. This is original upstream behavior (they disabled it). The user only asked for chelpers, ccomplexity, cinvariants extensions specifically. But they mentioned coctree is also a compiled extension (setup.py builds it). The user said "fast Cython extensions implemented (chelpers, ccomplexity, cinvariants)" and "compiled extensions (chelpers, ccomplexity, and cinvariants) should work in their original context from Python side." So coctree isn't in the must-work list, but it's still built and importable (we tested `coctree.angle_exceeds` works directly). 

However, the bare `from coctree import` in octree.py does cause a fallback only (no error). Should I fix it too for correctness? It's a latent bug similar to periodic.py. Since pyknotid doesn't actually use the cython functions there (commented out), fixing the import wouldn't change behavior — it would just make the fallback not needed if coctree were imported. But if we fix it, then `cangle_exceeds`/`cline_to_segments` would be non-None (since coctree is installed), yet they're never used because the assignment is commented out. So no behavioral change. I could fix it for cleanliness, but it's not needed. 

Actually wait — is `from coctree import` ever resolved? In the source tree during in-place builds, `from coctree import` works when `pyknotid/simplify` is in sys.path — but generally in the installed package it fails silently and falls back. Since behavior is unaffectedFrances, I'll leave it as-is to minimize changes... Actually, let me reconsider: The user is concerned with the three listed extensions. The bare import in octree.py silently falls back to Python; the compiled coctree extension works when imported directly (`from pyknotid.simplify import coctree`). That's fine.

All done
 final answer.
. Here's a summary of what I did.

## What was wrong

1. **NumPy 2.x removed the old aliases** `np.int`, `np.float`, `np.complex` (and `np.bool` is on its way out). These were used all over the Python code **and** inside the `.pyx` sources (`ccomplexity.pyx` used `dtype=np.int`).
2. **Python 3 removed `fractions.gcd`** — `pyknotid/make/torus.py` imported it at module import time, which blocked `import pyknotid.make`.
3. **A latent bare relative import** in `pyknotid/spacecurves/periodic.py` (`from periodiccell import ...`) broke `pyknotid.make.periodic_knot` outside the package directory.
4. **`python-planarity` 1.0.0 renamed its drawplanar vertex/edge attributes** (`vertex_position`/`vertex_start`/`vertex_end`, `edge_position`/`edge_start`/`edge_end`), while pyknotid 0.5.3 expects the old `pos`/`start`/`end` names. This made `reconstructed_space_curve()` / `draw_planar_graph()` raise `KeyError: 'pos'`.
5. The old Cython-generated C code is also incompatible with NumPy 2.x's removed C API, so the extensions had to be re-cythonised/recompiled with a modern Cython.

## Fixes applied (in `/app/pyknotid`)

- **NumPy aliases** (13 files): `n.int/np.int → n.int_/np.int_`, `n.float/np.float → n.float64`, `n.bool/np.bool → n.bool_`, `n.complex → n.complex128` (with `isinstance(…, complex)` kept as built-in checks to preserve the old alias semantics).
- **`pyknotid/make/torus.py`**: `from fractions import gcd` → `from math import gcd`.
- **`pyknotid/spacecurves/periodic.py`**: fixed the bare import to `from pyknotid.spacecurves.periodiccell import …`.
- **`pyknotid/representations/representation.py`**: normalise planarity ≥1.0 drawplanar attribute names back to `pos`/`start`/`end` so both old and new `planarity` versions work.
- Recompiled all four Cython extensions with **Cython 3.3.0** against NumPy 2.3.0 headers: `chelpers`, `ccomplexity`, `cinvariants`, and also `coctree` (built by setup.py).

## Build & install (global environment)

- Installed build prerequisites: `setuptools`, `wheel`, `Cython`, plus runtime deps (`peewee`, `sympy`, `networkx`, `planarity`, `appdirs`, `requests`, `tqdm`, `vispy`) and `pytest`.
- Built extensions and installed with:
  ```
  cd /app/pyknotid && python3 setup.py build_ext --inplace
  python3 -m pip install .   # into /usr/local/lib/python3.13/site-packages
  ```
- One note: my first `pip install --force-reinstall .` briefly upgraded NumPy to 2.5.2; I **restored NumPy 2.3.0** and reinstalled pyknotid afterward, so the installed extensions were built against NumPy 2.3.0.

## Verification

- The exact README snippet runs from the installed package with NumPy 2.3.0 and prints `alexander_polynomial(-1) = 6.999999999999999`.
- All loaded extensions resolve to the installed `.so` modules:
  - `/usr/local/lib/python3.13/site-packages/pyknotid/spacecurves/chelpers…so`
  - `/usr/local/lib/python3.13/site-packages/pyknotid/spacecurves/ccomplexity…so`
  - `/usr/local/lib/python3.13/site-packages/pyknotid/cinvariants…so`
  - `/usr/local/lib/python3.13/site-packages/pyknotid/simplify/coctree…so`
- All core tests pass, both from the source tree and against the installed package:
  - `tests/test_knot.py` + `tests/test_spacecurve.py` → **18 passed**
  - I verified Cython dispatch from real call sites: `chelpers` in `find_crossings`/gauss code, `ccomplexity` in `second_order_writhes` (prints `cython i1 …`), `cinvariants` in `vassiliev_degree_3`, and `coctree` directly (`angle_exceeds`).
- `tests/test_random_curves.py` and `tests/test_catalogue.py` were excluded per your instructions (dead code / database-dependent).
